# Measure theory — primary source acquisition and canonical-coverage harvest

Run: `subjects-01`, measure-theory scaffolding lane. Written by the source-acquisition
research subagent; this file is the source-of-record for what was actually obtained and
what each source's own headings are. Every heading list below was mechanically extracted
from the file named in its "obtained" line, not written from memory.

**Extraction method.** PDFs were downloaded with `curl` and their text extracted with
`pypdf` 6.15.0 in a throwaway venv (`/tmp/mtvenv`). Declaration sites were located by
line-anchored regex on the label + number, deduplicated, and **verified by gap analysis**:
for every counter in each primary source the extracted numbers form a complete run
`1..max` with no missing index (reported per section below). Statement text is the
source's own text, mechanically truncated; PDF text extraction mangles some mathematical
glyphs and inline display formulas, so the *headings and numbers* are exact and the
*statement snippets* are lossy-but-verbatim-derived. Ligature repair (fi/fl/ff) and
de-hyphenation were applied.

---

**Honest scoreboard.** Sources A, B, D, E: full text, harvested exhaustively.
Source F (Cohn): complete TOC to section level; no named-result harvest (text not obtained).
Source C (Folland): **no** primary text obtained. Its section titles come from two
independent course/syllabus witnesses (C2, C3) plus C4 for Ch. 7; its result numbering and
names come from C5, a set of notes that explicitly prints Folland's own numbering
(format `Theorem <notes-number>: <Folland-number>: <name>`). Four spot-checks against
independently known facts all agree (Folland 1.14 = the premeasure extension theorem,
2.14 = Monotone Convergence, 3.8 = Lebesgue–Radon–Nikodym, 7.2 = Riesz Representation).
**C5's own section numbering is NOT Folland's** — it inserts an extra §2.2 and merges
Folland's §3.2/§3.3 — so use C2/C3/C4 for section titles and C5 only for result numbers.

### Blockers

- **BLOCKER (minor).** Folland 2nd ed. primary text was not obtained from any
  publisher-, author- or library-hosted source; the Wiley product page carries no TOC and
  refuses automated fetch (403). Folland material below is therefore *secondary-witness*
  and must be treated as `literature-derived, secondary witness` — verify any exact
  Folland statement against the book before making it load-bearing.
- **BLOCKER (minor).** Cohn 2nd ed.: TOC only. No named-result harvest possible.
- **Partial.** Folland Ch. 7 numbering recovered for 7.1, 7.2, 7.5–7.9, 7.11–7.16, 7.18,
  7.19 only; 7.3, 7.4, 7.10, 7.17, 7.20+ were not reachable from C4.
- Rudin's text is **OCR**: numbers and headings are reliable, but the OCR mangles symbols
  (e.g. `σ`→`c`/`(7`, `L^1`→`I}`). Treat Rudin statement snippets as locators only.

## 1. SOURCE A — Tao, *An Introduction to Measure Theory* (GSM 126)

**Obtained: FULL TEXT.** Author's preliminary version, made available with AMS
permission; 265 PDF pages. Chapter/section range read for the harvest: **the whole book** —
front matter (the Notation section, which carries numbered results 0.0.1–0.0.6),
Chapter 1 §§1.1–1.7 in full, Chapter 2 §§2.1–2.4 in full.

### 1.1 Complete table of contents (verbatim, with the book's own page numbers)

```
Preface                                                        ix
Notation                                                        x
Acknowledgments                                               xvi
Chapter 1.  Measure theory                                      1
  §1.1.  Prologue: The problem of measure                       2
  §1.2.  Lebesgue measure                                      17
  §1.3.  The Lebesgue integral                                 46
  §1.4.  Abstract measure spaces                               79
  §1.5.  Modes of convergence                                 114
  §1.6.  Differentiation theorems                              131
  §1.7.  Outer measures, pre-measures, and product measures   179
Chapter 2.  Related articles                                  209
  §2.1.  Problem solving strategies                            210
  §2.2.  The Rademacher differentiation theorem                226
  §2.3.  Probability spaces                                    232
  §2.4.  Infinite product spaces and the Kolmogorov
         extension theorem                                     235
Bibliography                                                   243
Index                                                          245
```

Tao's in-section subheadings (not in the printed TOC but present in the body and used as
harvest anchors) include: §1.1.1 Elementary measure, §1.1.2 Jordan measure, §1.1.3
Connection with the Riemann integral; §1.2.1 Lebesgue outer measure, §1.2.2 Lebesgue
measurability, §1.2.3 Non-measurable sets; §1.3.1 Integration of simple functions, §1.3.2
Measurable functions, §1.3.3 Unsigned Lebesgue integrals, §1.3.4 Absolute integrability,
§1.3.5 Littlewood's three principles; §1.4.1 Boolean algebras, §1.4.2 σ-algebras and
measurable spaces, §1.4.3 Countably additive measures and measure spaces, §1.4.4
Measurable functions and integration, §1.4.5 The convergence theorems; §1.6.1 The
Lebesgue differentiation theorem in one dimension, §1.6.2 The Lebesgue differentiation
theorem in higher dimensions, §1.6.3 Almost everywhere differentiability; §1.7.1 Outer
measures and the Carathéodory extension theorem, §1.7.2 Pre-measures, §1.7.3 Lebesgue–
Stieltjes measure, §1.7.4 Product measures.

**No gaps anywhere.** The harvest below is therefore complete for Tao: 206 numbered
results and 237 numbered exercises, 443 headings.

> **Disposition note for the scaffolder.** In Tao a large fraction of the mathematical
> content lives in the *exercises* — he says so in the Preface ("many of the key results
> and examples in the subject will in fact be presented through the exercises"). An
> exercise carrying a parenthetical title is a named result and needs a disposition
> exactly like a theorem.

## 2. SOURCE B — Bass, *Real Analysis for Graduate Students*, Version 5.0

**Obtained: FULL TEXT.** Author-hosted, free, 480 PDF pages. Title page reads
"Real analysis for graduate students / Version 5.0 / Richard F. Bass / October 31, 2024";
copyright page records: first edition 2011, second edition 2013, Version 3.1 (2016),
4.1 (Mar 2020), 4.2 (Jun 2020), 4.3 (Jul 2022), **5.0 (Oct 2024)**. Cite the version, not
an "edition".

**Chapter/section range read for the harvest:** the whole book, Chapters 1–26. The
measure-theory half the commission names is **Chapters 2–17**; Chapters 18–26 (Banach
spaces, Hilbert spaces, topology, probability, harmonic functions, Sobolev spaces,
singular integrals, spectral theory, distributions) are harvested at heading level only.

### 2.2 Numbering integrity check

Bass runs **two counters per chapter**: one shared by
Theorem/Proposition/Lemma/Corollary/Definition/Example/Remark/Notation, and one for
Exercises. Extraction used three passes — strict line-anchored, then two progressively
looser ones for declarations whose line was polluted by a stray floated glyph
(`∗Theorem 4.6`, `P∞ R P∞Exercise 7.4`, `bExercise 16.2`, `thExercise 25.10`). On the
final harvest, **every chapter's counter forms a complete `1..max` run with no missing
index**, for both counters, Chapters 1–26.

Totals: **364 numbered results** (149 in Chapters 1–17) and **419 numbered exercises**
(231 in Chapters 2–17); **783 headings**.

### 2.3 Bass — full harvest of numbered headings

Chapters 1–17 carry statement snippets; Chapters 18–26 are heading-only (outside the
measure-theory scope but listed so nothing is silently dropped). Section markers
(`*§n.m Title*`) are the book's own section headings, located in the body text.

- **Definition 5.1** A function f : X → R is measurable or A measurable if {x : f(x) > a}∈A for all a ∈ R. A complex-valued function is measurable if both its real and imaginary parts are measurable. 
- **Example 5.2** Suppose f is real-valued and identically constant. Then the set {x : f(x) > a} is either empty or all of X, so f is measurable.

Analysis qualifier syllabus, whose first line reads "The course text was Real Analysis by
Folland") and C3 (Georgia Tech MATH 6337 course page), cross-checked against each other;
Chapter 7's from C4 (Stony Brook MAT 533 notes on "Folland's Real Analysis: Chapter 7").
Result numbers and names are from C5, which prints Folland's own number for each result.

### 3.1 Chapter list (2nd ed.)

0. Prologue (set theory, orderings, cardinality, the extended reals, metric spaces)
1. Measures · 2. Integration · 3. Signed Measures and Differentiation ·
4. Point Set Topology · 5. Elements of Functional Analysis · 6. L^p Spaces ·
7. Radon Measures · 8. Elements of Fourier Analysis · 9. Elements of Distribution Theory ·
10. Topics in Probability Theory · 11. More Measures and Integrals

### 3.2 Section titles, verbatim from the witnesses

**Chapter 1. Measures** (C2 and C3 agree)
- 1.1 Introduction — C3 gives "Measure: introduction"; C2 adds "including the construction of a non-measurable set"
- 1.2 σ-algebras (C3: "Sigma Algebras")
- 1.3 Measures
- 1.4 Outer measures (C3: "Outer Measure: Carathéodory's Theorem")
- 1.5 Borel measures on R (C3: "Borel Measure on the Real Line")

**Chapter 2. Integration**
- 2.1 Measurable functions
- 2.2 Integration of non-negative functions
- 2.3 Integration of functions (C2 note: "mostly focused on real-valued"); Folland's own title is "Integration of Complex Functions"
- 2.4 Modes of convergence
- 2.5 Product measures
- 2.6 The n-dimensional Lebesgue integral (C3)
- 2.7 Integration in polar coordinates (C3)

**Chapter 3. Signed measures and differentiation**
- 3.1 Signed measures
- 3.2 The Lebesgue–Radon–Nikodym theorem
- 3.3 *(not on C2's exam list; Folland's Complex Measures — inferred by position, NOT witnessed)*
- 3.4 Differentiation on Euclidean space
- 3.5 Functions of bounded variation

**Chapter 5. Elements of functional analysis** (C2)
- 5.1 Normed vector spaces · 5.2 Linear functionals · 5.3 Baire category theorem and
  consequences · 5.4 (weak and weak\* topologies; C2 cites "theorem 5.18") · 5.5 Hilbert spaces

**Chapter 6. L^p spaces** (C2 for 6.1–6.3; 6.4/6.5 titles from C5's TOC, which follows Folland)
- 6.1 Basic theory of L^p spaces · 6.2 The dual of L^p · 6.3 Some useful inequalities
  (C2 restricts to Chebyshev's inequality) · 6.4 Distribution functions and weak L^p ·
  6.5 Interpolation of L^p spaces

**Chapter 7. Radon measures** (C4, verbatim)
- 7.1 Positive linear functionals on C_c(X)
- 7.2 Regularity and approximation theorems
- 7.3 The dual of C_0(X)
- 7.4\* Products of Radon measures
- 7.5 Notes and References

> Note: "vague convergence" is treated **inside 7.3**, not as its own section — a common
> misremembering. C4 records Prop. 7.19 (vague convergence and distribution functions)
> under the 7.3 material.

### 3.3 Folland Ch. 7 — the numbered results C4 actually witnesses

- **Prop. 7.1** If I is a positive linear functional on C_c(X), then for each compact K ⊂ X there is a constant C_K with |I(f)| ≤ C_K‖f‖_u for all f ∈ C_c(X) with supp(f) ⊂ K.
- **Theorem 7.2 (The Riesz Representation Theorem)** If I is a positive linear functional on C_c(X), there is a unique Radon measure μ on X with I(f) = ∫ f dμ for all f ∈ C_c(X); moreover μ(U) = sup{I(f) : f ∈ C_c(X), f ≺ U} for U open and μ(K) = inf{I(f) : f ∈ C_c(X), f ≥ χ_K} for K compact.
  *(C4 prints this theorem's statement without a number, but numbers its two displayed
  equations (7.3) and (7.4), places it immediately after Prop. 7.1, and refers back to
  "Theorem 7.2" in the uniqueness argument — so the number 7.2 is inferred, not read off a
  heading. Confirm against the book before citing the number.)*
- **Prop. 7.5** Every Radon measure is inner regular on all of its σ-finite sets.
- **Cor. 7.6** Every σ-finite Radon measure is regular; if X is σ-compact, every Radon measure on X is regular.
- **Prop. 7.7** If μ is a σ-finite Radon measure on X and E a Borel set: (a) for every ε > 0 there exist open U and closed F with F ⊂ E ⊂ U and μ(U∖F) < ε; (b) there exists an F_σ set A and a G_δ set B with A ⊂ E ⊂ B and μ(B∖A) = 0.
- **Thm 7.8** If X is an LCH space in which every open set is σ-compact (e.g. X second countable), then every Borel measure on X that is finite on compact sets is regular and Radon.
- **Prop. 7.9** If μ is a Radon measure on X, then C_c(X) is dense in L^p(μ) for 1 ≤ p < ∞.
- **Prop. 7.11** (lower semicontinuity toolkit) χ_U is LSC for U open; cf is LSC for c ∈ [0,∞) and f LSC; a supremum of a family of LSC functions is LSC.
- **Prop. 7.12** If G is a family of nonnegative LSC functions on an LCH space X directed upward and f = sup G, then ∫ f dμ = sup_{g∈G} ∫ g dμ for μ Radon.
- **Cor. 7.13** If μ is Radon and f ≥ 0 is LSC, then ∫ f dμ = sup{∫ g dμ : g ∈ C_c(X), 0 ≤ g ≤ f}.
- **Prop. 7.14** If μ is Radon and f a nonnegative Borel measurable function, then ∫ f dμ = inf{∫ g dμ : g ≥ f, g LSC} = sup{∫ g dμ : 0 ≤ g ≤ f, g USC with compact support}.
- **Lemma 7.15** If I ∈ C_0(X,R)\*, there exist positive linear functionals I^± ∈ C_0(X,R)\* with I = I^+ − I^−.
- **Prop. 7.16** A complex Borel measure μ is Radon iff |μ| is Radon; M(X) is a vector space and μ ↦ ‖μ‖ is a norm on it.
- **Cor. 7.18** If X is a compact Hausdorff space, C(X)\* is isometrically isomorphic to M(X).
- **Prop. 7.19** For μ, {μ_n} ⊂ M(X) with F_n(x) = μ_n((−∞,x]) and F(x) = μ((−∞,x]): (a) if sup‖μ_n‖ < ∞ and F_n → F at every continuity point of F, then μ_n → μ vaguely; (b) if μ_n ≥ 0 and μ_n → μ vaguely, …

*Not recovered from C4: 7.3, 7.4, 7.10, 7.17, 7.20 and beyond.*

**Treat the numbers and names as reliable and the transcribed text as a locator only.**

## 4. SOURCE D — Rudin, *Real and Complex Analysis*, 3rd ed. (McGraw-Hill, 1987)

**Obtained: FULL TEXT (OCR).** Internet Archive full-text stream of the 3rd International
Edition (1987); 976 KB of OCR'd text. Rudin numbers **every** display —
definitions, theorems, remarks, examples — in a single running counter per chapter, and
places the number *before* the label ("2.14 Theorem"). Range read: Chapters 1–9 in full;
Chapters 10–20 are complex analysis and out of scope.

### 4.1 The two numbers the commission asked to pin down — CONFIRMED verbatim

- **2.14 Theorem** *(the Riesz representation theorem)* — "Let X be a locally compact
  Hausdorff space, and let Λ be a positive linear functional on C_c(X). Then there exists a
  σ-algebra 𝔐 in X which contains all Borel sets in X, and there exists a unique positive
  measure μ on 𝔐 which represents Λ in the sense that Λf = ∫_X f dμ for every f ∈ C_c(X)…"
  The chapter TOC entry is **"The Riesz representation theorem 40"**.
- **6.10 The Theorem of Lebesgue–Radon–Nikodym** — "Let μ be a positive σ-finite measure
  on a σ-algebra 𝔐 in a set X, and let λ be a complex measure on 𝔐. …"
  The chapter TOC entry is **"Consequences of the Radon-Nikodym theorem 124"** (§) —
  the theorem itself sits under "Absolute continuity 120".

## 5. SOURCE E — Stein & Shakarchi, *Real Analysis: Measure Theory, Integration, and Hilbert Spaces* (Princeton Lectures in Analysis III, 2005)

**Obtained: FULL TEXT**, 423 PDF pages, from a course-hosted copy (see ledger row E).
Range read: the whole book; Chapters 1, 2, 3 and 6 are the measure-theory core.

S&S number results **`⟨section⟩.⟨index⟩` within each chapter** — so "Theorem 3.4" means
*chapter's §3, fourth result*, and the same number recurs in every chapter. Always cite
chapter **and** number.

"Exercise 2.5.6", "Example 2.5.2", "Theorem 2.5.4" — all sighted in the book's own
Introduction, which was obtained with the front matter).

**Scaffolding note.** Cohn §3.3's title — "Definition of L^p **and 𝓛^p**" — is itself the
convention flag: Cohn keeps the space of functions (𝓛^p) and the space of a.e.-equivalence
classes (L^p) as separate named objects. See the convention ledger.

---

## 7. Convention ledger — where the sources actually disagree

Each row records what each source *does*, with a locator. Verified against the obtained
text for A (Tao), B (Bass), D (Rudin), E (Stein–Shakarchi); Folland rows are marked
`[secondary]` and Cohn rows are TOC-level inference marked `[TOC]`.

### 7.1 What "measurable" means

| source | primary sense of "measurable set" | Carathéodory condition |
|---|---|---|
| **Tao** | **Two senses, in two different places.** Concretely in R^d: `Definition 1.2.2 (Lebesgue measurability)` — *E ⊆ R^d is Lebesgue measurable if for every ε>0 there is an open U ⊇ E with m\*(U∖E) ≤ ε* (outer approximation by open sets, not Carathéodory). Abstractly: `Definition 1.4.1`/`1.4.12` — "*We sometimes say that E is B-measurable, or measurable with respect to B, if E ∈ B*" (membership in a given Boolean/σ-algebra). | Introduced **later and separately** as `Definition 1.7.2 (Carathéodory measurability)`: µ\*(A) = µ\*(A∩E) + µ\*(A∖E) for every A ⊆ X. Tao deliberately does *not* found Lebesgue measure on it. |
| **Bass** | Membership in the ambient σ-algebra 𝒜 (`Definition 3.1` fixes (X,𝒜)); for functions, `Definition 5.1`: *f : X → R is measurable or 𝒜-measurable if {x : f(x) > a} ∈ 𝒜 for all a ∈ R*. | `Definition 4.5`: *A ⊂ X is µ\*-measurable if µ\*(E) = µ\*(E∩A) + µ\*(E∩A^c) for all E ⊂ X*; `Theorem 4.6` (Bass calls this "the Carathéodory theorem", warning it is not the Carathéodory *extension* theorem of §4.5). Lebesgue measure IS built this way. |
| **Stein–Shakarchi** | Ch. 1 §3: Lebesgue measurable via outer approximation by open sets, like Tao. | Deferred to Ch. 6 §1.1 "Exterior measures and Carathéodory's theorem". |
| **Folland** `[secondary]` | Membership in a given σ-algebra ℳ. | §1.4 "Outer measures", witnessed by C3 as "Outer Measure: Carathéodory's Theorem"; the extension theorem is Folland **1.14**. |
| **Rudin** | `1.18 Definition`: a positive measure is a function on a σ-algebra 𝔐; measurable = member of 𝔐. Rudin's `2.14` Riesz theorem *produces* the σ-algebra 𝔐 rather than assuming Borel. | Present in the Riesz construction, not stated as a stand-alone named notion. |
| **Cohn** `[TOC]` | §1.1 Algebras and Sigma-Algebras, §1.3 Outer Measures. | §1.3. |

**Scaffolding consequence.** "Measurable" is genuinely ambiguous across the corpus. Any
library item must say *which* σ-algebra, or say "Carathéodory-measurable for µ\*"
explicitly. The equivalence of Tao's `Definition 1.2.2` with Carathéodory-measurability
for Lebesgue outer measure is a **theorem**, not a definition, and it needs proving.

### 7.2 Range of a measure; signed measures

- **Tao** `Definition 1.4.27`: an (unsigned) countably additive measure is µ : ℬ → **[0,+∞]**.
  He works throughout in the "non-negative theory" vs "absolutely integrable theory" split
  he announces in `Remark 0.0.1`. **Tao's book contains no signed measures at all** (0 hits
  for "signed measure"), hence no Hahn/Jordan decomposition and no Radon–Nikodym theorem.
- **Bass** `Definition 3.1`: µ : 𝒜 → **[0,∞]**. `Definition 12.1`: a **signed measure** is
  µ : 𝒜 → **(−∞,∞]** — i.e. `+∞` is allowed, `−∞` is not, and the series must converge
  absolutely when the union has finite measure. Bass then writes "positive measure" when he
  wants to stress non-negativity.
- **Rudin** `1.18 Definition`: a *positive measure* has range in **[0,∞]** and is countably
  additive, plus the non-triviality convention that µ(A) < ∞ for at least one A ∈ 𝔐;
  a *complex measure* is complex-valued (hence **never** takes ±∞), and Rudin's Ch. 6 is
  built on complex measures rather than extended-real signed ones.
- **Folland** `[secondary]`: signed measures in §3.1; Folland 3.8 is the
  **Lebesgue–Radon–Nikodym theorem** stated for a σ-finite **signed** ν and σ-finite
  positive µ.
- **Cohn** `[TOC]`: Ch. 4 "Signed and Complex Measures", §4.1.

**Disagreement to flag:** Bass allows a signed measure to take `+∞`; Rudin's complex
measures cannot take any infinite value; Folland allows signed with at most one infinity.
A library statement of Jordan decomposition or Radon–Nikodym must fix this explicitly.

### 7.3 Extended reals for *functions*

- **Tao**: yes, pervasively. The Notation chapter defines `[0,+∞]` with `+∞·0 = 0`, notes
  multiplication is *upward* continuous but not downward, and says explicitly that this
  asymmetry "will ultimately cause us to define integration from below rather than from
  above". `Definition 1.3.8` has unsigned measurable f : R^d → **[0,+∞]**.
- **Bass**: `Definition 6.1` states the `a_i = 0, µ(E_i) = ∞ ⟹ a_iµ(E_i) = 0` convention
  for simple functions; measurable functions in `Definition 5.1` are real-valued, with
  extended-real values admitted where needed.
- **Rudin**: `1.18`ff. "Arithmetic in [0, ∞]" is its own TOC section in Ch. 1.
- All sources adopt `0·∞ = 0`. No disagreement found.

### 7.4 "Borel measure" and local finiteness; "Radon"; regularity

- **Rudin** `2.15 Definition`, verbatim: "*A measure µ defined on the σ-algebra of all Borel
  sets in a locally compact Hausdorff space X is called a Borel measure on X. If µ is
  positive, a Borel set E ⊆ X is outer regular or inner regular, respectively, if E has
  property (c) or (d) of Theorem 2.14. If every Borel set in X is both outer and inner
  regular, µ is called regular.*" So for Rudin, **"Borel measure" carries NO finiteness
  requirement**, and "regular" demands inner regularity on **every** Borel set. Rudin then
  says in the same paragraph that this is too strong in general: his `2.14` gives outer
  regularity on all E ∈ 𝔐 but inner regularity only on open sets and on sets of finite
  measure. `2.16 Definition` supplies σ-compact / σ-finite, and notes E ∈ 𝔐 of σ-finite
  measure is inner regular. `2.17 Theorem` recovers full regularity when X is σ-compact.
- **Folland** `[secondary, C4 verbatim]`: "*A Radon measure on X is a Borel measure that is
  finite on all compact sets, outer regular on all Borel sets, and inner regular on all
  open sets.*" Radon measures are therefore on the **Borel** σ-algebra, and inner
  regularity is demanded **only on open sets** — weaker than Rudin's "regular". Folland
  `Prop. 7.5` then upgrades: every Radon measure is inner regular on all of its σ-finite
  sets; `Cor. 7.6`: every σ-finite Radon measure is regular.
- **Bass** §17.3, verbatim: µ is "*regular if µ(E) = inf{µ(G) : G open, E ⊆ G} and
  µ(E) = sup{µ(K) : K compact, K ⊆ E} for all measurable E*" — the strong two-sided form,
  and Bass only proves it in the setting his Riesz theorem uses: `Theorem 17.3` takes
  **X a compact metric space** and produces a finite measure on the Borel σ-algebra ℬ,
  with `Proposition 17.6` giving the regularity. Bass never introduces the word "Radon".
- **Cohn** `[TOC]`: §1.5 "Completeness and Regularity"; §7.4 "Additional Properties of
  Regular Measures"; §7.5 "The µ\*-Measurable Sets and the Dual of L^1" — i.e. Cohn keeps
  the **µ\*-measurable** σ-algebra, larger than Borel, as a named object distinct from
  Borel. This is the clearest source for "Radon on Borel vs on a larger σ-algebra".

**Three genuinely different objects share the name "Riesz representation theorem":**
Rudin `2.14` (positive functional on C_c(X), X LCH, produces a *complete* σ-algebra 𝔐 ⊇ Borel);
Folland `7.2` (positive functional on C_c(X), produces a unique **Radon** measure on Borel);
Bass `17.3` (positive functional on C(X), X **compact metric**, produces a finite Borel measure)
and Bass `17.8` (bounded functional on C(X) ↦ finite **signed** Borel measure);
Rudin also has a second one in Ch. 6 ("The Riesz representation theorem", p. 129, for
bounded functionals on C_0(X)); Cohn §7.2; and Stein–Shakarchi Ch. 4 §5.1 is the
*Hilbert-space* Riesz representation theorem, an entirely different statement.
**Never cite "the Riesz representation theorem" without the source and number.**

### 7.5 L^p: functions or equivalence classes?

- **Bass** §15.1, verbatim: "*L^p is a normed linear space. This is not quite right. The L^p
  norm of a function satisfies all the properties of a norm except that ‖f‖_p = 0 does not
  imply that f is the zero function, only that f = 0 a.e.*" — he then passes to
  "*equivalence classes with respect to this equivalence relation, and define ‖f‖_p to be
  the L^p norm of any function in the same equivalence class as f*", and says he keeps
  this "in the back of our minds". So Bass: **classes, but introduced as a repair.**
- **Cohn** `[TOC]`: §3.3 is titled "**Definition of L^p and 𝓛^p**" and §3.4 "Properties of
  L^p and 𝓛^p" — Cohn keeps the two objects **separately named throughout**. This is the
  source to follow if the library wants the distinction to be visible.
- **Rudin** `1.30 Definition` defines L^1(µ) as a collection of *functions*; the quotient is
  taken later in Ch. 3.
- **Folland** `[secondary]` §6.1 "Basic theory of L^p spaces": classes.
- **Tao**: `Definition 1.3.17` defines ‖f‖_{L^1(R^d)} for almost-everywhere-defined
  functions and works with a.e.-defined functions throughout, so the quotient is implicit.
- **Disagreement is real.** Any library L^p item must say whether its elements are functions
  or classes, because completeness (Riesz–Fischer) and the "‖f‖_p = 0 ⟹ f = 0" axiom
  depend on it.

### 7.6 How the integral is built

- **Tao** builds **from below, via unsigned simple functions**, in three named steps:
  `Definition 1.3.3` (integral of an unsigned simple function),
  `Definition 1.3.12` (**lower** unsigned Lebesgue integral, defined for *every* unsigned
  function, measurable or not, as sup over simple minorants),
  `Definition 1.3.13` (unsigned Lebesgue integral := the lower one, for measurable f).
  He motivates the "from below" choice in the Notation chapter from the upward-but-not-
  downward continuity of multiplication on `[0,+∞]`. The abstract repetition is
  `Definition 1.4.35`/`1.4.37`. **Tao's "region under the graph" material is the Riemann/
  Jordan story (`Exercise 1.1.25`, area interpretation), not his definition of the Lebesgue
  integral** — do not attribute a layer-cake definition to him.
- **Bass** `Definition 6.1`: ∫s dµ = Σa_iµ(E_i) for nonnegative simple s, then
  ∫f dµ = sup{∫s dµ : 0 ≤ s ≤ f, s simple}, then ∫f = ∫f^+ − ∫f^− provided not both are
  infinite. Identical shape to Tao's abstract construction.
- **Folland** `[secondary]` §2.2/§2.3: simple functions, same shape.
- **Stein–Shakarchi** build in **four graded stages** (Ch. 2 §1): simple functions →
  bounded functions supported on a set of finite measure (`Theorem 1.4`, bounded
  convergence) → nonnegative measurable (Fatou `Lemma 1.7`, MCT `Corollary 1.9`) →
  general integrable. This staging is visible in their numbering and is a real expositional
  difference, not a cosmetic one.
- **Cohn** `[TOC]` §2.3 "The Integral"; his Introduction sketches the range-partition
  (Lebesgue-sum) motivation before defining the integral in §2.3.

### 7.7 Two further conventions worth pinning before authoring

- **Interval convention in the construction of Lebesgue measure.** Bass builds
  Lebesgue–Stieltjes measure from **half-open (a,b]** intervals ("it is technically simpler
  to work with intervals of the form (a,b] rather than open intervals", Ch. 4 preamble),
  and his `Example 4.3`/`4.4` set ℓ((a,b]) = b − a and α(b) − α(a). Tao builds from **boxes**
  (`Definition 1.1.1`) and Stein–Shakarchi from **closed cubes** (Ch. 1 §2). The resulting
  measures agree; the intermediate lemmas do not transfer verbatim.
- **Naming of the extension theorem.** Tao `Theorem 1.7.8` is the **Hahn–Kolmogorov
  theorem** (premeasure ⟶ measure); Bass §4.5 calls the corresponding result **the
  Carathéodory extension theorem** and explicitly warns not to confuse it with his
  `Theorem 4.6` ("sometimes known as the Carathéodory theorem"); Folland's is `1.14`
  `[secondary]`; Stein–Shakarchi Ch. 6 §1.3 "The extension theorem". **The same theorem has
  three names across the two primary sources.**

### 7.8 Coverage gap that determines source assignment

Grep counts over the obtained full texts (case-insensitive, whole book):

| phrase | Tao | Bass |
|---|---|---|
| "signed measure" | **0** | 38 |
| "Hahn decomposition" | **0** | 9 |
| "Radon-Nikodym" | 1 (a passing mention) | 28 |
| "Riesz representation" | 2 (passing) | 27 |
| "Lp space" | **0** | 15 |
| "Fubini" | 18 | 37 |

**Tao's book stops before signed measures, the Hahn/Jordan decompositions,
Radon–Nikodym, L^p theory and Riesz representation** — he defers them to *An epsilon of
room, Vol. I*. So a Tao-only pair cannot be "backed by two independent treatments" for any
of those topics: Bass (Ch. 12, 13, 15, 17), Folland (Ch. 3, 6, 7), Rudin (Ch. 3, 6) and
Cohn (Ch. 3, 4, 7) are the load-bearing backing there. Conversely Tao is the richer source
for Jordan measure, the Riemann/Jordan prologue, modes of convergence and the
differentiation theorems.

### Standing cautions for whoever scaffolds from this file

1. The two commissioned URLs (Tao `measure-book1.pdf`, Bass `real.html`) are **404**.
   Use the URLs in the acquisition ledger, and record *those* in `sources.references`.
2. Bass is a **versioned** living document (v5.0, 2024-10-31). Cite the version and date;
   a numbered result may move between versions.
3. Statement snippets below are PDF-extraction output. Numbers, labels and parenthetical
   names are exact; the mathematics inside a snippet is mangled by the extractor and is a
   **locator, not a quotation**. Re-read the source before quoting any statement into an
   item's `[F#]`/`[A#]`/`[L#]` fact.
4. Folland must not be made load-bearing on the strength of this file alone (§3, BLOCKER).
5. Tao carries no signed-measure/Radon–Nikodym/L^p/Riesz material (§7.8) — do not plan a
   pair on those topics with Tao as one of its two required independent treatments.
